Table 7.1
:
Various models of communication complexity. The three possible communication patterns — simultaneous, one-way, and two way — are shown in figure
7.1
.

A simple question we can ask is: what are the minimal quantum resources needed to provide an advantage, for some function, over the standard classical class
\mathsf{R}2
?
If another class can be shown to contain a function not in
\mathsf{R}2
, we say it has been separated from
\mathsf{R}2
.
In fact, proving such functional separations is difficult.
Instead, what we usually aim for is to find a
partial function
in the other class but not in
\mathsf{R}2
.
A partial function is similar to a function, but its value is only defined for some of the inputs.
Another way to phrase this is to say that we provide a promise on the inputs, so that the inputs where the partial function isn’t defined never appear.

The question of finding the weakest quantum class separated (by a partial function) from
\mathsf{R}2
has been intensely studied, with a series of successive results gradually reducing the quantum resources needed to find an advantage.
These results are summarized in figure
7.2
.
The weakest quantum class separated from
\mathsf{R}2
is
\mathsf{R}\|^{*}
, which allows shared entanglement and simultaneous classical communication.
We will prove this separation here; it turns out that non-local quantum computation techniques are key to the proof.
This is pretty strong: the communication model is the weakest one (simultaneous), and the communication is only classical.
The only quantum resource we need is initial shared entanglement.

\mathsf{R}\|^{*}
\mathsf{Q}\|
\mathsf{Q}\|^{*}
\mathsf{Q}1
\mathsf{R}1
\mathsf{R}2
\mathsf{Q}2
\mathsf{R}\|
59
44
26
27
;
33
;
9
28
10
32

Figure 7.2
:
Quantum versus Classical Communication. Here, an arrow from
A
to
B
denotes that
A
exponentially outperforms
B
for some task. We use
2
to denote interactive protocols,
1
to denote one-way protocols and
\|
to denote simultaneous protocols. The red arrow uses NLQC techniques and is the focus of this section.

The proof of this separation has two components.
After choosing a partial function, we need to 1) prove that function is not in
\mathsf{R}2
and 2) give a communication protocol showing it can be done efficiently in
\mathsf{R}\|^{*}
.
The function we will use is called
forrelation
, which we describe later.
Part 1), putting a classical lower bound on the communication cost of forrelation, is quite difficult and not discussed here, but see
33
.
Our main interest is in establishing the second part, which connects to non-local computation.
In fact we will find that the non-local quantum computation technique of chapter
6
provides the needed tool to design a protocol for forrelation in
\mathsf{R}\|^{*}
.

7.2
Transforming
\mathsf{Q}\|^{*}
protocols into
\mathsf{R}\|^{*}
protocols

Consider an arbitrary
\mathsf{Q}\|^{*}
protocol.
This has the form shown in figure
7.1(c)
, with the messages allowed to be quantum.
Although not shown there, we also allow Alice and Bob to share entanglement.
We can always view the referee’s actions as first applying a unitary
U
and then measuring the first qubit to determine
f(x,y)
.
In this section, we show a technique to convert such protocols into
\mathsf{R}\|^{*}
protocols, that is, to make the communication classical.
When
U
has low
T
-depth, this transformation is efficient.

The basic idea behind our transformation is as follows.
We start with a
\mathsf{Q}\|^{*}
protocol, and then apply the
T
-depth based NLQC protocol to the operation applied by the referee.
The crucial observation is that before Alice and Bob communicate in the
T
-depth NLQC protocol, Alice already holds
P^{(k)}U{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
with
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
the input and
U
the unitary being applied.
Because of this, we can have Alice measure the first qubit and obtain a measurement outcome
m^{\prime}
, which will be related in a simple way to the measurement outcome the referee would have obtained, call it
m
.
In particular if
b
is a bit fixing if there is an
X
correction on the first qubit in
P^{(k)}U{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
, then we get that
m=m^{\prime}\oplus b
.
Then, we just need to look at Speelman’s protocol and determine how many bits Alice and Bob need to send to determine
b
.
This is worked out in the next theorem.

Theorem 22

Consider a
\mathsf{Q}\|^{*}
protocol for function
f
, which uses
m
qubits of message.
Suppose that this protocol involves the referee applying a
T
-depth-
d
unitary to the messages received from Alice and Bob, along with at most
a
qubits of ancilla and then measuring the first qubit to return the output.
Then there is a
\mathsf{R}\|^{*}
protocol for
f
which uses
O((68(m+a))^{d})
qubits of communication and entanglement.

Proof.
To begin, suppose Alice and Bob have already executed their own actions in the
\mathsf{Q}\|^{*}
protocol, and now hold message system
M_{A}
and
M_{B}
.
Then, considering the unitary
U_{M_{A}M_{B}E}
the referee would apply to their systems (
E
represent any ancilla the referee uses), Alice and Bob execute the first round operations in Speelman’s NLQC protocol for
U_{M_{A}M_{B}E}
.
After doing so, Alice holds the state

\displaystyle X^{\vec{g}_{d}(x,y)}Z^{\vec{h}_{d}(x,y)}U_{M_{A}M_{B}E}{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}

(7.1)

Note that we’ve adopted the notation from chapter
6
to describe the Pauli corrections.
Alice now measures the first qubit, obtaining outcome

\displaystyle m^{\prime}=m\oplus g_{d,1}

(7.2)

where
m
will be distributed as if a measurement was made on
U_{M_{A}M_{B}E}{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
.
Alice will send the referee
m^{\prime}
, and then we need to understand the (simultaneous, classical) communication complexity of Alice and Bob computing
g_{d,1}
.
One way to do this is for Alice and Bob to send the referee all of their measurement outcomes from all teleportations used in the instantiation of Speelman’s protocol.
From theorem
21
, the number of such teleportations is
O((K(M+a))^{d})
, where
M
is the number of message qubits and
a
is the number of ancilla qubits.
Using the improved analysis from
62
, we can take
K=68
.

Using the value of
m^{\prime}
and
g_{d,1}
, the referee can compute
m
, which recall is distributed exactly like the referee’s measurement outcome in the original protocol.
Thus if the
\mathsf{Q}\|^{*}
protocol was correct with probability
p
, then the
\mathsf{R}\|^{*}
protocol will be correct with probability
p
as well.

7.3
Separating
\mathsf{R}\|^{*}
and
\mathsf{R}2

The transformation given in the last section can be used to give a partial function separation of
\mathsf{R}\|^{*}
and
\mathsf{R}2
.
Our starting point will be earlier results that show
\mathsf{Q}\|^{*}
contains partial functions not in
\mathsf{R}2
.
In particular, we use the partial function constructed in
33
, which is called
forrelation
.
Another function constructed in
9
would also work equally well, but we focus on one example for simplicity.

To describe the forrelation problem, let
n
be a power of 2.
Define the forrelation of a string
x\in\{-1,1\}^{n}
as

\displaystyle\text{forr}(x):=\frac{1}{n}{\left\langle{x_{1}}\right|}H^{\otimes n/2}{\left|{x_{2}}\right\rangle}

(7.3)

where
x_{1}
is the first half of
x
and
x_{2}
is the second half of
x
.
Define a communication problem as follows.

Definition 23

Alice gets
x\in\{-1,1\}^{n}
and Bob gets
y\in\{-1,1\}^{n}
, where
n
is a power of 2. The goal of the players is to output
f(x,y)
defined by

\displaystyle f(x,y)=\begin{cases}-1\quad\text{if}\,\,\text{forr}(x\cdot y)\geq\alpha\\
+1\quad\text{if}\,\,\text{forr}(x\cdot y)\leq\alpha/2\end{cases}

(7.4)

where
\alpha>0
is a constant. Here,
x\cdot y
denotes the point-wise product of
x
and
y
.

This is a partial function because of the promise we give on the forrelation of the inputs.

It was shown in
33
that forrelation is not in
\mathsf{R}2
, and further they gave a
\mathsf{Q}\|^{*}
protocol which uses
O(\text{poly}(\log(n)))
communication and has constant
T
depth.
Further, the protocol doesn’t involve the referee using any ancilla.
Consequently, the transformation in theorem
22
immediately gives a
O(\text{poly}(\log(n)))
cost
\mathsf{R}\|^{*}
protocol, so we are done.

We won’t review the
\mathsf{Q}\|^{*}
protocol we start with here, but see
33
for details.

Part III
Lower bounds

Chapter 8
Lower bounds for unitaries

In part
II
of these notes we discussed upper bound techniques for NLQC, including the garden-hose and code-routing protocols for
f
-routing, the general purpose
T
-depth based technique, and the port-teleportation upper bound.
In this part we begin the study of entanglement lower bounds for NLQC.

We can see already, given our upper bound techniques, that we shouldn’t expect to be able to prove very strong lower bounds.
To see why, recall that in the context of
f
-routing we showed, for instance,

\displaystyle E(f)\leq\text{FormulaSize}(f)

(8.1)

so that entanglement lower bounds on
f
-routing also imply lower bounds on formula size.
This also holds for other complexity measures, including span program size, a measure we mentioned briefly in chapter
3
.
In classical complexity theory very little is known about lower bounds on these measures, so if we do too well at proving entanglement lower bounds, we would make complexity theory breakthroughs.
As well,
T
-depth lower bounds seem to be hard to prove in quantum complexity theory as well — the best such bound known is of
T
-depth 2 — so entanglement lower bounds on any unitaries would also imply impressive quantum complexity statements.

In this chapter we will focus on two simple lower bound techniques which apply to unitaries.
These lower bound techniques prove, at best, linear lower bounds, so don’t run into any complexity barriers.
Nonetheless, linear lower bounds such as these already have useful applications in quantum position-verification and quantum gravity, among other areas.
We will explore the gravity connection in chapter
11
.

8.1
From entropy to entanglement

This chapter focuses on lower bounding the entanglement cost of implementing
unitaries
as NLQCs.
The same techniques do not seem to apply easily to more general quantum channels.
One of the properties that makes unitaries easier to lower bound is that they are extreme points in the convex space of quantum channels.
This is a key ingredient in showing that lower bounds against pure state resources can be upgraded to lower bounds against general (mixed) resource states.
In other words, classical correlation doesn’t help to implement extreme points in the space of quantum channels, so in some sense we can reduce to the simpler setting of studying entanglement in pure states.

In this section we will first develop the general argument showing that, for unitary channels, a lower bound on entropy in pure state resources gives a lower bound on entanglement in any mixed state resource.
We start by recalling some tools for quantifying correlation and entanglement in quantum systems.

8.1.1
Correlation and entanglement measures

This section can be skipped and returned to when lemmas recorded here are referenced later.

The mutual information:
The mutual information quantifies correlations in quantum states.
It is defined as follows.

Definition 24

Given a density matrix
\rho_{AB}
, the mutual information
I(A:B)_{\rho}
is defined by

\displaystyle I(A:B)_{\rho}=S(A)_{\rho}+S(B)_{\rho}-S(AB)_{\rho}.

(8.2)

The mutual information is continuous in the choice of quantum state
70
, as expressed in the next lemma.

Lemma 25

Suppose that
\|\rho-\sigma\|_{1}=\epsilon
.
Then

\displaystyle|I(A:B)_{\rho}-I(A:B)_{\sigma}|\leq 4n_{A}\epsilon+(1+2\epsilon)h\left(\frac{2\epsilon}{1+2\epsilon}\right).

(8.3)

where
h(x)=-x\log x-(1-x)\log(1-x)
is the binary entropy function.

The mutual information also can’t grow too much when adding subsystems, as expressed in the next lemma.

Lemma 26

The mutual information satisfies

\displaystyle I(A:BC)_{\rho}\leq I(A:B)_{\rho}+2S(C)_{\rho}.

(8.4)

This follows from subadditivity and the Araki-Lieb inequality.

We also make use of the conditional quantum mutual information,

\displaystyle I(A:B|C)_{\rho}=S(AC)_{\rho}+S(BC)_{\rho}-S(C)_{\rho}-S(ABC)_{\rho}.

(8.5)

The mutual and conditional mutual informations are related by the chain rule,

\displaystyle I(A:BC)_{\rho}=I(A:B|C)_{\rho}+I(A:C)_{\rho}.

(8.6)

We have the following statement about the conditional mutual information.

Lemma 27

The quantum conditional mutual information satisfies the data processing inequality,

\displaystyle I(A:B|C)_{\rho}\geq I(A:B|C)_{\mathcal{N}_{B}(\rho)}.

(8.7)

Proof.
This is more commonly stated for the mutual information (corresponding to
C=\varnothing
) but the statement for the conditional mutual information follows immediately,

\displaystyle I(A:B|C)_{\rho}

\displaystyle=I(A:BC)_{\rho}-I(A:C)_{\rho}

\displaystyle\geq I(A:BC)_{\mathcal{N}_{B}(\rho)}-I(A:C)_{\rho}

\displaystyle=I(A:BC)_{\mathcal{N}_{B}(\rho)}-I(A:C)_{\mathcal{N}_{B}(\rho)}

\displaystyle=I(A:B|C)_{\mathcal{N}_{B}(\rho)}.

(8.8)

We use the chain rule in the first line, data processing for the mutual information in the second line, the fact that the channel doesn’t act on
C
in the third line, and the chain rule again in the last line.

The entanglement of formation:
The entanglement of formation
35
;
71
is a tool for quantifying entanglement, which we will make use of in this chapter.

Definition 28

The
entanglement of formation
is defined as

\displaystyle E_{f}(A:B)_{\rho}=\min_{\{p_{i},{\left|{\psi_{i}}\right\rangle}\}}\sum_{i}p_{i}S(B)_{\psi_{i}},

(8.9)

where the minimization is over ensembles
\{p_{i},{\left|{\psi_{i}}\right\rangle}\}
such that
\rho=\sum_{i}p_{i}|\psi_{i}\rangle\!\langle\psi_{i}|
.

Note that an equivalent definition would replace
S(B)_{\psi_{i}}
with
S(A)_{\psi_{i}}
above.
The entanglement of formation is a faithful measure of entanglement, meaning that it is zero if and only if
\rho
is separable.
This is easy to see from its definition: if it is separable so that

\displaystyle\rho_{AB}=\sum_{i}p_{i}\rho_{A}^{i}\otimes\rho^{i}_{B}

(8.10)

then we introduce decompositions
\rho_{A}^{i}=\sum_{k}\lambda_{k}^{i}|\phi_{k}^{i}\rangle\!\langle\phi_{k}^{i}|_{A}
and
\rho_{B}^{i}=\sum_{k}\mu_{k}^{i}|\varphi_{k}^{i}\rangle\!\langle\varphi_{k}^{i}|_{B}
and we see that
E_{f}(A:B)_{\rho}=0
.
Conversely, if
E_{f}(A:B)_{\rho}=0
then there exists a decomposition into states
\psi_{i}
such that
S(B)_{\psi_{i}}=0
for all
i
, which means all
\psi_{i}
are tensor product, and hence
\rho
is separable.

The entanglement of formation satisfies the following property, which shows that it can’t grow too much as you add subsystems.

Lemma 29

The entanglement of formation satisfies

\displaystyle E_{f}(A:BC)_{\rho}\leq E_{f}(A:B)_{\rho}+\log d_{C}.

(8.11)

This statement follows from the definition of the entanglement of formation, subadditivity of the von Neumann entropy, and the statement
S(X)\leq\log d_{X}
.

We also have a data processing inequality for the entanglement of formation
15
.

Lemma 30

The entanglement of formation is non-increasing under the action of a local quantum channel,
1
1

1

In fact the entanglement of formation is also decreasing under LOCC operations, although we will not need that stronger property here.

\displaystyle E_{f}(A:B)_{\rho_{AB}}\geq E_{f}(A:B^{\prime})_{\mathcal{I}_{A}\otimes\mathcal{N}_{B\rightarrow B^{\prime}}(\rho_{AB})}.

(8.12)

Finally, we recall a Fannes type continuity bound
58
;
70
for the entanglement of formation.

Lemma 31

Consider two states
\rho_{AB}
,
\sigma_{AB}
with
\|\sigma_{AB}-\rho_{AB}\|_{1}\leq\epsilon
, define
\eta_{\epsilon}=2\sqrt{\epsilon(1-\epsilon)}
and
d=\min\{d_{A},d_{B}\}
.
Then, the entanglement of formation of
\rho_{AB}
and
\sigma_{AB}
cannot be too different:

\displaystyle|E_{f}(A:B)_{\rho}-E_{f}(A:B)_{\sigma}|

\displaystyle\leq\eta_{\epsilon}\log d+H(\eta_{\epsilon})

(8.13)

where
H(x)=(1+x)h\left(\frac{x}{1+x}\right)
and
h(x)=-x\log x-(1-x)\log(1-x)
.

8.1.2
Entanglement lower bounds from entropy lower bounds

We claim that once we’ve lower bounded the entropy of any resource state needed to implement a unitary as an NLQC, then we’ve automatically lower bounded the entanglement, as quantified by the entanglement of formation.
To prove this, we will need a lemma stating that if a convex mixture of quantum channels approximates a unitary, then, on average, each term in the convex mixture must be close to the unitary.

To prove this lemma, we define an average case notion of the fidelity, which captures how well a quantum channel preserves information on average.

Definition 32

The average case fidelity of a quantum channel
\mathcal{N}_{A}
is defined as

\displaystyle\bar{F}(\mathcal{N}_{A})=\int d\psi\,F(\psi_{A},\mathcal{N}_{A}(\psi))

(8.14)

where the integral is over the Haar measure.

Then, we need the following statement.

Lemma 33

Given a quantum channel
\mathcal{N}
acting on a
d
dimensional space, we have

\displaystyle\frac{d+1}{d}\left(1-\bar{F}(\mathcal{N})\right)\leq\frac{1}{2}\|\mathcal{N}-\mathcal{I}\|_{\diamond}\leq\sqrt{d(d+1)}\sqrt{1-\bar{F}(\mathcal{N})}.

(8.15)

This is proposition 9 in
67
.

Now we are ready to prove the following lemma regarding unitary channels.

Lemma 34

Suppose that
\{\mathcal{N}^{i}\}_{i}
are quantum channels, let
U
be a unitary, and let
\mathcal{U}(\cdot)=U(\cdot)U^{\dagger}
be a unitary channel.
Then if
\|\sum_{i}p_{i}\mathcal{N}^{i}-\mathcal{U}\|_{\diamond}\leq\epsilon
, then we have that
\sum_{i}p_{i}\|\mathcal{N}^{i}-\mathcal{U}\|_{\diamond}\leq d\sqrt{2\epsilon}
.

Proof.
We have by assumption that

\displaystyle\epsilon\geq\left\|\sum_{i}p_{i}\mathcal{N}^{i}-\mathcal{U}\right\|_{\diamond}.

(8.16)

We would like to bound a similar quantity but with the sum moved outside the diamond norm.
To do this, we first relate the above to the average case fidelity,

\displaystyle\epsilon\geq\left\|\sum_{i}p_{i}\mathcal{N}^{i}-\mathcal{U}\right\|_{\diamond}=\left\|\sum_{i}p_{i}\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{N}^{i}-\mathcal{I}\right\|_{\diamond}\geq 2\frac{d+1}{d}\left(1-\bar{F}\left(\sum_{i}p_{i}\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{N}^{i}\right)\right).

(8.17)

Now we use that the average case fidelity is linear, in the sense that

\displaystyle\bar{F}\left(\sum_{i}p_{i}\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{N}^{i}\right)

\displaystyle=\int d\psi\,F\left(\sum_{i}p_{i}\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{N}^{i}(\psi),\psi\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}p_{i}\int d\psi F\left(\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{N}^{i}(\psi),\psi\right)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}p_{i}\bar{F}(\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{N}^{i})

(8.18)

The first equality used that the integral is over pure states.
Returning to equation (
8.17
), we have now

\displaystyle\epsilon\geq 2\frac{d+1}{d}\left(1-\sum_{i}p_{i}\bar{F}\left(\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{N}^{i}\right)\right).

(8.19)

or equivalently,

\displaystyle\sum_{i}p_{i}\bar{F}\left(\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{N}^{i}\right)\geq 1-\frac{1}{2}\frac{d}{d+1}\epsilon

(8.20)

Now we consider the quantity we want to upper bound, which is
\sum_{i}p_{i}\|\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{N}^{i}-\mathcal{I}\|_{\diamond}
,

\displaystyle\sum_{i}p_{i}\|\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{N}^{i}-\mathcal{I}\|_{\diamond}\leq 2\sqrt{d(d+1)}\sum_{i}p_{i}\sqrt{1-\bar{F}(\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{N}^{i})}

(8.21)

Now, use that
f(x)=\sqrt{1-x}
is concave to move the sum inside the square root, to obtain

\displaystyle\sum_{i}p_{i}\|\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{N}^{i}-\mathcal{I}\|_{\diamond}\leq 2\sqrt{d(d+1)}\sqrt{1-\sum_{i}p_{i}\bar{F}(\mathcal{U}^{\dagger}\circ\mathcal{N}^{i})}\leq\sqrt{2}d\sqrt{\epsilon}

(8.22)

as claimed.

Now, we’re ready to prove the following lemma.

Theorem 35

Suppose that any
\epsilon
-correct (in diamond norm) implementation of unitary
U_{AB}
as an NLQC using a pure resource state
{\left|{\Psi}\right\rangle}_{LR}
must have
S(R)_{\Psi}\geq\lambda-F(\epsilon)
, with
F(x)
a concave, monotone increasing, function. Then, any protocol using a mixed state resource
\Psi_{LR}
must satisfy
E_{f}(L:R)_{\Psi}\geq\lambda-F(d_{AB}\sqrt{2\epsilon})
.

Proof.
By assumption, we have that the channel implemented by the NLQC, call it
\mathcal{N}
, is
\epsilon
-close to the target unitary channel,

\displaystyle\|\mathcal{N}-\mathcal{U}\|_{\diamond}\leq\epsilon.

(8.23)

Let the resource system used in the NLQC be denoted
\Psi_{LR}
.
Let

\displaystyle\Psi_{LR}=\sum_{i}p_{i}|\Psi^{i}\rangle\!\langle\Psi^{i}|_{LR}

(8.24)

be any ensemble decomposition of
\Psi_{LR}
into pure states.
Then, the channel
\mathcal{N}
is given by
\mathcal{N}=\sum_{i}p_{i}\mathcal{N}^{i}
where
\mathcal{N}^{i}
is the channel implemented by the NLQC protocol when given pure state
{\left|{\Psi^{i}}\right\rangle}
as a resource state.
We have then that

\displaystyle\left\|\sum_{i}p_{i}\mathcal{N}^{i}-\mathcal{U}\right\|_{\diamond}\leq\epsilon.

(8.25)

Now from lemma
34
, we get that we can pull the sum out of the diamond norm at the expense of a relaxed error,

\displaystyle\sum_{i}p_{i}\|\mathcal{N}^{i}-\mathcal{U}\|_{\diamond}\leq d_{AB}\sqrt{2\epsilon}.

(8.26)

Label
\|\mathcal{N}^{i}-\mathcal{U}\|_{\diamond}=\epsilon_{i}
, so that
\sum_{i}p_{i}\epsilon_{i}\leq 2d\sqrt{\epsilon}
.
Now consider the entanglement of formation.

\displaystyle E_{f}(L:R)_{\Psi}

\displaystyle=\min_{p_{i},\Psi^{i}}\sum_{i}p_{i}S(R)_{\Psi^{i}}

\displaystyle\geq\min_{p_{i},\Psi^{i}}\sum_{i}p_{i}\left(\lambda-F(\epsilon_{i})\right)

\displaystyle=\lambda-\min_{p_{i},\Psi^{i}}\sum_{i}p_{i}F(\epsilon_{i})

\displaystyle\geq\lambda-F(d\sqrt{2\epsilon})

(8.27)

where in the first line we used that the channel implemented when using resource state
\Psi^{i}
is
\epsilon_{i}
close to
\mathcal{U}
, and in the last line we used that
F
is concave and monotone increasing.

The requirement that
F
is concave and monotone increasing may seem somewhat specific and unlikely to be satisfied in practice.
However, the monotone increasing property just expresses that the lower bound should weaken as the error increases, so is expected.
The concavity property holds in practice in the two settings where we apply this below.

8.2
Lower bound from controllable correlation

Given a channel
U_{AB}
, what properties of the unitary could lower bound the entanglement cost?
For inspiration, return to the simple example of routing that we studied in chapter
2
.
There, we have a quantum input
A
on the left and a classical bit
b
on the right.
The bit
b
should control where
A
ends up:
A
should go left if
b=0
, and go right if
b=1
.

To lower bound this, we considered placing
A
in a correlated state with a reference, which we call
\bar{Q}
.
We labelled the quantum systems produced on the right in the first round by
R
.
Without lowering how well the protocol works, we can always send all of
R
to the side labelled by
b
since there is no output required on the other side.
Meanwhile the system kept on the left we label
A
, and the system sent from left to right we label
B
.
Then we noticed that completing the routing task implies that

\displaystyle I(\bar{Q}:AR)_{b=0}=2\log d_{\bar{Q}}

\displaystyle I(\bar{Q}:BR)_{b=1}=2\log d_{\bar{Q}}

(8.28)

From this starting point, we used entropic inequalities to lower bound
S(R)
, which also lower bounds the entropy of the portion of the resource system held on the right.

\mathcal{V}^{L}
\mathcal{V}^{R}
\mathcal{W}^{R}
\mathcal{W}^{L}
L
R
A
B
A
B
M_{1}
M_{2}
M_{3}
M_{4}
Q

Figure 8.1
:
A non-local quantum computation implementing a unitary
U_{AB}
. To prove lower bounds on the entanglement cost, we consider placing the input system
A
in a state
P_{QA}
correlated with a reference system
Q
. We indicate this with the dashed line. The state
P_{QA}
need not be pure. We find that if adjusting the input on
B
changes the amount of correlation between
A
and
Q
in the final state, that
L:R
must be entangled.

The key property of the routing task we make use of is that
the input on the right controls where the correlation with
\bar{Q}
goes.

This occurs even though
A
, which begins correlated with
\bar{Q}
, is on the left and causally separated from
B
.
To address the entanglement cost in a more general setting, we will take this as our starting point.
We want to capture the extent to which a quantum channel can redirect the correlation in
A
shared with
\bar{Q}
, in a way controlled by the input on
B
.
The property this defines we will call the
controllable correlation
, which we then prove lower bounds the entanglement in the resource system.

In fact, we will modify this idea a little bit, and look at when the correlation between
\bar{Q}
and
A
can be made either small or large by adjusting the input on
B
.
This is equivalent when we have pure state inputs and a unitary channel because in that case the output is pure which means,

\displaystyle I(\bar{Q}:A)+I(\bar{Q}:B)=2S(\bar{Q})

(8.29)

So
I(\bar{Q}:A)
small is equivalent to
I(\bar{Q}:B)
large.
This formulation turns out to be more convenient, and to be more similar to the next lower bound we present.

For convenience, we will switch our system labels to those shown in figure
8.1
.
We then make the following definition.

Definition 36

Consider a unitary
U_{AB}
and choose a state on
\bar{Q}A
, which we label
P_{\bar{Q}A}
, with
n_{\bar{Q}}=n_{A}
.
Define the states

\displaystyle\rho^{1}_{\bar{Q}AB}

\displaystyle=U_{AB}(P_{\bar{Q}A}\otimes\phi^{1}_{B})U_{AB}^{\dagger},

\displaystyle\rho^{2}_{\bar{Q}AB}

\displaystyle=U_{AB}(P_{\bar{Q}A}\otimes\phi^{2}_{B})U_{AB}^{\dagger}.

(8.30)

We say that
U_{AB}
has
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
-controllable correlation if there exist states
\phi^{1}_{B},\phi^{2}_{B}
, and
P_{\bar{Q}A}
such that

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}=I(\bar{Q}:A)_{\rho^{1}},\qquad\lambda_{2}=I(\bar{Q}:A)_{\rho^{2}}

(8.31)

with
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}
. If there is no choice of states
P_{\bar{Q}A}
,
\phi^{1}_{B},\phi^{2}_{B}
such that
\lambda_{1}>\lambda_{2}
we say
U_{AB}
is not controllably correlated.

As a simple example, the CNOT
B→A
gate is controllably correlated: choose for instance
P_{\bar{Q}A}=(\rho_{cc})_{\bar{Q}A}=\frac{1}{2}(|00\rangle\!\langle 00|_{\bar{Q}A}+|11\rangle\!\langle 11|_{\bar{Q}A})
. Taking first the control on
B
to be
{\left|{0}\right\rangle}
, CNOT
B→A
acts identically on
A
, leaving
\bar{Q}A
in the maximally classically correlated state
P_{\bar{Q}A}
, so
\lambda_{1}=1
.
On the other hand choosing the input on
B
to be the maximally mixed state erases the state on
A
and leaves it product with
\bar{Q}
, so
\lambda_{2}=0
.
In contrast, the SWAP
AB
gate is not controllably correlated — regardless of the input on
B
, the final state on
\bar{Q}A
will be product, so we always have
\lambda_{1}=\lambda_{2}=0
.
Similarly, the identity is not controllably correlated since
P_{\bar{Q}A}
will not be influenced by the input on
B
.

With this notion of controllable correlation in hand, we can prove the following theorem, which lower bounds the entropy of one end of the resource state.
We can then employ theorem
35
to upgrade this to a lower bound on the entanglement of formation in the resource state.

Theorem 37

Suppose that unitary
U_{AB}
has
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
-controllable correlation.
If an NLQC protocol using a pure resource state
{\left|{\Psi}\right\rangle}_{LR}
gives an
\epsilon
-correct (in diamond norm distance with
\epsilon<1/4
) implementation of
U_{AB}
, then

\displaystyle S(R)_{\Psi}\geq\frac{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}{2}-\Delta(2\sqrt{\epsilon},n_{A})

(8.32)

where

\displaystyle\Delta(x,n)=4nx+(1+2x)h\left(\frac{2x}{1+2x}\right).

(8.33)

Proof.
Recall that we defined the states

\displaystyle\rho^{1}_{\bar{Q}AB}

\displaystyle=U_{AB}(P_{\bar{Q}A}\otimes\phi^{1}_{B})U_{AB}^{\dagger},

\displaystyle\rho^{2}_{\bar{Q}AB}

\displaystyle=U_{AB}(P_{\bar{Q}A}\otimes\phi^{2}_{B})U_{AB}^{\dagger}.

(8.34)

These are the states resulting from the exact implementation of the unitary
U_{AB}
.
When
U_{AB}
is replaced by the
\epsilon
-close implementation, we label the resulting states as
\sigma^{1}
and
\sigma^{2}
, and note that we have

\displaystyle\|\rho^{1}_{\bar{Q}AB}-\sigma^{1}_{\bar{Q}AB}\|_{1}\leq\epsilon,

\displaystyle\|\rho^{2}_{\bar{Q}AB}-\sigma^{2}_{\bar{Q}AB}\|_{1}\leq\epsilon,

(8.35)

which follows from the definition of the diamond norm distance.
Note further that we write
\sigma_{\bar{Q}M_{1}M_{2}}^{1,2}
for the states produced mid-way through the NLQC protocol (see figure
8.1
) upon giving input
\phi^{1,2}
.
As well, we will drop the state label when considering the entropy of
\bar{Q}
, since this is unaffected by the state on
B
.
Thus
S(\bar{Q})=S(\bar{Q})_{\sigma^{1}}=S(\bar{Q})_{\sigma^{2}}
.

We wish to understand how systems
M_{1}
and
M_{2}
are related to system
\bar{Q}
.
First observe that by the causal structure of the circuit,

\displaystyle\sigma_{M_{2}\bar{Q}}^{i}=\sigma_{M_{2}}^{i}\otimes\rho_{\bar{Q}}.

(8.36)

This holds regardless of the input on
B
, so for both
\sigma^{1}
and
\sigma^{2}
.
We will use this below in the form

\displaystyle\boxed{S(M_{2}\bar{Q})_{\sigma^{1}}=S(M_{2})_{\sigma^{1}}+S(\bar{Q})_{\sigma^{1}}.}

(8.37)

Next, consider
M_{1}
.
We have by assumption that

\displaystyle I(\bar{Q}:A)_{\rho^{2}}=\lambda_{2}.

(8.38)

We need to undo the last step of the NLQC circuit so as to construct the state on
M_{1}M_{2}
from that on
A
.
To do this, we consider taking a dilation of the channel
\mathcal{W}^{L}
applied on Alice’s side in the second round, and label the resulting unitary by
W^{L}_{M_{1}M_{2}\rightarrow AE}
where
E
is the ancillary system produced by the unitary.
This produces a density matrix
\sigma^{2}_{\bar{Q}AE}
.
We claim this is close to
\rho^{2}_{QA}\otimes\kappa_{E}
for some choice of density matrix
\kappa_{E}
.
To see why, recall that

\displaystyle\|\sigma_{\bar{Q}AB}-\rho_{\bar{Q}AB}\|_{1}\leq\epsilon

(8.39)

Considering the extension of
\sigma_{\bar{Q}AB}
to
\sigma_{\bar{Q}ABE}
, we apply lemma
75
to find that there exists an extension of
\rho_{\bar{Q}AB}
such that

\displaystyle\|\sigma_{\bar{Q}ABE}-\rho_{\bar{Q}ABE}\|_{1}\leq 2\sqrt{\epsilon}

(8.40)

But, then notice that
\rho_{\bar{Q}AB}
is a pure state.
This means every extension must be of the form
\rho_{\bar{Q}AB}\otimes\kappa_{E}
for some density matrix
\kappa_{E}
, so then

\displaystyle\|\rho_{\bar{Q}A}^{2}\otimes\kappa_{E}-\sigma^{2}_{\bar{Q}AE}\|_{1}\leq 2\sqrt{\epsilon}.

(8.41)

The state on
\bar{Q}M_{1}M_{2}
then satisfies

\displaystyle\|(W^{L}_{M_{1}M_{2}\rightarrow AE})^{\dagger}(\rho_{\bar{Q}A}^{2}\otimes\kappa_{E})W^{L}_{M_{1}M_{2}\rightarrow AE}-\sigma^{2}_{\bar{Q}M_{1}M_{2}}\|_{1}\leq 2\sqrt{\epsilon}.

(8.42)

Consider the state as above produced on giving input
\phi^{2}_{B}
, and consider the mutual information
I(\bar{Q}:M_{1})_{\sigma^{2}}
,

\displaystyle I(\bar{Q}:M_{1})_{\sigma^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq I(\bar{Q}:M_{1}M_{2})_{\sigma^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq I(\bar{Q}:M_{1}M_{2})_{\rho^{2}}+\Delta(\sqrt{2\epsilon},n_{\bar{Q}})

\displaystyle=I(\bar{Q}:A)_{\rho^{2}}+\Delta(\sqrt{2\epsilon},n_{\bar{Q}})

\displaystyle=\lambda_{2}+\Delta(2\sqrt{\epsilon},n_{\bar{Q}}).

(8.43)

The first inequality uses data processing, the second inequality uses equation (
8.42
) and the continuity statement lemma
25
and the third uses that
\rho_{\bar{Q}M_{1}M_{2}}=(W^{L}_{M_{1}M_{2}\rightarrow AE})^{\dagger}(\rho_{\bar{Q}A}^{2}\otimes\kappa_{E})W^{L}_{M_{1}M_{2}\rightarrow AE}
.

Finally, notice that the state on
M_{1}\bar{Q}
cannot depend on the input on
B
, so that

\displaystyle I(\bar{Q}:M_{1})_{\sigma^{1}}=I(\bar{Q}:M_{1})_{\sigma^{2}}\leq\lambda_{2}+\Delta(2\sqrt{\epsilon},n_{\bar{Q}}).

(8.44)

This statement is key to our proof and worth commenting on.
This is telling us that even when input state
\phi^{1}_{B}
, the correlation across
Q\,:M_{1}
must be small, and in particular similar to its value when inputting
\phi^{2}_{B}
.
But, when the input state is
\phi^{1}_{B}
, a lot of correlation has to end up in
A
.
This means
M_{1}M_{2}
is highly correlated with
Q
even while
M_{1}
is not.
We will use this below in the form

\displaystyle\boxed{S(M_{1}\bar{Q})_{\sigma^{1}}\geq S(M_{1})_{\sigma^{1}}+S(\bar{Q})_{\sigma^{1}}-\lambda_{2}-\Delta(2\sqrt{\epsilon},n_{\bar{Q}})}.

(8.45)

Continuing, we make use of the statement

\displaystyle I(\bar{Q}:A)_{\rho^{1}}=\lambda_{1}.

(8.46)

To do so, we first use continuity of the mutual information to turn this into a statement about
\sigma^{1}
,

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}-\Delta(\epsilon,n_{\bar{Q}})\leq I(\bar{Q}:A)_{\sigma^{1}}.

(8.47)

Then observe that from data processing,

\displaystyle I(\bar{Q}:A)_{\sigma^{1}}\leq I(\bar{Q}:M_{1}M_{2})_{\sigma^{1}}

(8.48)

so then

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}-\Delta(\epsilon,n_{\bar{Q}})\leq S(M_{1}M_{2})_{\sigma^{1}}+S(\bar{Q})_{\sigma^{1}}-S(M_{1}M_{2}\bar{Q})_{\sigma^{1}}

(8.49)

or, rearranging,

\displaystyle\boxed{S(M_{1}M_{2}\bar{Q})_{\sigma^{1}}\leq S(M_{1}M_{2})_{\sigma^{1}}+S(\bar{Q})-\lambda_{1}+\Delta(\epsilon,n_{\bar{Q}}).}

(8.50)

which we will use below.

Now, consider the conditional mutual information
I(M_{1}:M_{2}|\bar{Q})_{\sigma^{1}}
.
This is

\displaystyle I(M_{1}:M_{2}|\bar{Q})_{\sigma^{1}}

\displaystyle=S(M_{1}\bar{Q})_{\sigma^{1}}+S(M_{2}\bar{Q})_{\sigma^{1}}-S(\bar{Q})_{\sigma^{1}}-S(M_{1}M_{2}\bar{Q})_{\sigma^{1}}\qquad\,\,\,\qquad\qquad\qquad\text{definition of CMI}

\displaystyle=S(M_{1}\bar{Q})_{\sigma^{1}}+S(M_{2})_{\sigma^{1}}-S(M_{1}M_{2}\bar{Q})_{\sigma^{1}}\,\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\quad\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\qquad\text{eq. \eqref{eq:M2}}

\displaystyle\geq S(M_{1})_{\sigma^{1}}+S(\bar{Q})+S(M_{2})_{\sigma^{1}}-\lambda_{2}-S(M_{1}M_{2}\bar{Q})_{\sigma^{1}}-\Delta(2\sqrt{\epsilon},n_{\bar{Q}})\quad\quad\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{eq. \eqref{eq:M1}}

\displaystyle\geq S(M_{1})_{\sigma^{1}}+S(M_{2})_{\sigma^{1}}-S(M_{1}M_{2})_{\sigma^{1}}+\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-\Delta(\epsilon,n_{\bar{Q}})-\Delta(2\sqrt{\epsilon},n_{\bar{Q}})\quad\,\,\,\,\text{eq. \eqref{eq:fixed}}

\displaystyle\geq\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-2\Delta(2\sqrt{\epsilon},n_{\bar{Q}})\,\,\,\,\quad\qquad\qquad\qquad\qquad\,\,\,\qquad\qquad\quad\qquad\,\qquad\qquad\text{subadditivity}

so that the conditional mutual information is bounded below. In the last line we used that
\Delta(2\sqrt{\epsilon},n_{\bar{Q}})\geq\Delta(\epsilon,n_{\bar{Q}})
to simplify the error term.

Next, we would like to translate this to a bound on the mutual information of the resource state.
Using that

\displaystyle\sigma_{\bar{Q}M_{1}M_{2}}^{1}=\mathcal{V}^{L}_{AL\rightarrow M_{1}}\otimes\mathcal{V}^{R}_{RB\rightarrow M_{2}}(\sigma^{1}_{\bar{Q}ALRB})

(8.51)

and data processing for the CMI (lemma
27
) we have that

\displaystyle I(M_{1}:M_{2}|\bar{Q})_{\sigma^{1}_{M_{1}M_{2}\bar{Q}}}\leq I(RA:LB|\bar{Q})_{\sigma_{\bar{Q}ALRB}^{1}}=I(L:R)_{\Psi}

(8.52)

where in the second equality we used that

\displaystyle\sigma^{1}_{\bar{Q}ALRB}=P_{\bar{Q}A}\otimes\Psi_{LR}\otimes\phi^{1}_{B}.

(8.53)

Combined with our lower bound on the CMI, we have then

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}-2\Delta(2\sqrt{\epsilon},n_{\bar{Q}})\leq I(R:L)_{\Psi}.

(8.54)

Considering in particular a pure state resource, this leads to

\displaystyle S(R)_{\Psi}\geq\frac{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}{2}-\Delta(2\sqrt{\epsilon},n_{\bar{Q}})

(8.55)

as claimed.

Using this lower bound on the entropy along with theorem
35
that lets us upgrade lower bounds on entropy to lower bounds on entanglement (when considering unitaries), we obtain the following.

Corollary 38

Suppose that unitary
U_{AB}
has
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
-controllable correlation. Then, any NLQC protocol which implements
U_{AB}

\epsilon
-correctly must use a resource state with

\displaystyle E_{f}(L:R)_{\Psi}\geq\frac{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}{2}-\Delta(2\sqrt{d_{AB}\sqrt{2\epsilon}},n_{A}).

(8.56)

Proof.
This follows immediately from theorem
37
, theorem
35
, and the fact that
\Delta(x,n)
is concave in its first argument.

Example applications:
Now that we have our first general purpose technique for lower bounding entanglement in NLQC, we can start trying it out on some commonly encountered gates.

For the CZ gate, we saw that choosing
P_{\bar{Q}A}=\Psi^{+}_{\bar{Q}A}
,
\phi^{1}_{B}=|0\rangle\!\langle 0|_{B}
, and
\phi^{2}_{B}=|+\rangle\!\langle+|_{B}
gives
\lambda_{1}=2
,
\lambda_{2}=1
, so a lower bound of
1/2
on the entanglement of formation in the resource state.
More generally, we can choose any unitary
U
and then numerically optimize the choice of inputs
\phi^{1}_{B},\phi^{2}_{B}
to obtain as strong of a lower bound as we can.
The results of doing such an optimization are shown in table
8.2
.
We also include results from a second technique, based on a quantity we define called the “controllable entanglement”.
You can ignore those bounds for now and return to them after reading the next section.

:
Results of a numerical optimization computing the controllable entanglement and controllable correlation for some simple gates; these values are lower bounds on the entanglement of formation in any resource state that suffices to complete the corresponding gate as an NLQC. The reference state is the choice of state on
\bar{Q}A
used in deriving the lower bound; see figure
8.1
.
\rho_{cc}
refers to the maximally classically correlated pair of qubits, while
\Psi^{+}
is a Bell state. Matrix expressions for the listed gates appear in appendix
A.2
.

To build up some further intuition for when this technique works well, we can try running our optimization procedure on random choices of unitary.
The result of doing so for 100,000 random two qubit unitaries is shown in figure
8.3
.
The most striking feature of the resulting data is that we never encounter a unitary where this technique fails to return a positive lower bound.
We know such examples exist — for instance the SWAP or identity unitaries — but they seem to be rare.

Figure 8.3
:
Histogram showing the value of the controllable correlation lower bound computed for
100,000
two qubit unitaries drawn from the Haar distribution. The average value of the lower bound is
\approx 0.230
.

These observations let us start to get intuition for which unitaries require entanglement and which don’t.
Apparently most do, and our only known examples that do not are the SWAP and identity.
An interesting open problem is to characterize if these are the only examples that don’t require entanglement (up to equivalence under local unitaries).

8.3
Lower bound from controllable entanglement

In the last section we proved a lower bound of
1/2
on the entanglement of formation needed to implement a
CNOT
gate as an NLQC.
Our best upper bound for implementing
CNOT
comes from the teleportation based protocol seen in section
6.1
, which uses 1 EPR pair, which has
E_{f}=1
.
Our upper and lower bounds don’t quite match, which is a bit unsatisfying, especially considering that the
CNOT
is probably the simplest possible (non-trivial) unitary we could consider.
Can we improve our bound, and get a lower bound of
E_{f}\geq 1
?

In this section we give such an improved lower bound.
The technique is a little bit harder than the controllable correlation lower bound.
In particular we will need to invoke some results on Schumacher compression, which are reasonably involved.

To motivate our second lower bound technique, let’s revisit the
CNOT
gate.
Consider the set-up we’ve shown in figure
8.1
, where we take
CNOT_{A\rightarrow B}
to act on
{\left|{\Psi^{+}}\right\rangle}_{\bar{Q}A}
.
Choosing the input on
B
to be
{\left|{+}\right\rangle}_{B}
, we find that
\bar{Q}\!:\!A
is left maximally entangled, so
\lambda_{1}=I(\bar{Q}\!:\!A)_{\rho^{1}}=2
.
Choosing the input on
B
to be
{\left|{0}\right\rangle}_{B}
, we find that
\bar{Q}:A:B
is placed in a GHZ state, and
\bar{Q}\!:\!A
becomes a maximally classically correlated state
\frac{1}{2}(|00\rangle\!\langle 00|_{\bar{Q}A}+|11\rangle\!\langle 11|_{\bar{Q}A})
.
This gives
\lambda_{2}=1
.
This leads to the lower bound of
E_{f}\geq(2-1)/2=1/2
for the
CNOT
.

To improve this, a natural idea is to try and use a measure of
entanglement
, rather than correlation, between
\bar{Q}
and
A
: while the correlation as measured by the mutual information drops to half its maximal value, if we measure entanglement the correlation measure would drop from maximal to zero, since
\bar{Q}:A
is only classically correlated in the GHZ state.
Perhaps this behaviour would lead to a tighter bound.

This motivates the following definition.

Definition 39

Consider a unitary
U_{AB}
. We say that
U_{AB}
has
\lambda
-
controllable entanglement
if there exists states
\phi^{1}_{B}
,
\phi^{2}_{B}
such that

\displaystyle\rho^{1}_{\bar{Q}AB}=U_{AB}(\Psi^{+}_{\bar{Q}A}\otimes\phi^{1}_{B})U^{\dagger}_{AB},\qquad\rho^{2}_{\bar{Q}AB}=U_{AB}(\Psi^{+}_{\bar{Q}A}\otimes\phi^{2}_{B})U^{\dagger}_{AB}

(8.57)

with

\displaystyle\lambda_{1}:=E_{f}(\bar{Q}:A)_{\rho^{1}}>0,\qquad 0=E_{f}(\bar{Q}:A)_{\rho^{2}}.

(8.58)

If there are no such choices of input state, we say that
U_{AB}
is not controllably entangled.

This notion of the controllable entanglement leads to the following lower bound.

Theorem 40

Suppose that an NLQC protocol using resource state
\Psi_{LR}
implements a unitary
U_{AB}
to within diamond norm distance
\gamma
, and where
U_{AB}
has
\lambda
-controllable entanglement.
Then for small enough
\gamma
, we have that

\displaystyle E_{f}(L:R)_{\Psi}\geq\lambda-P(d_{AB}\sqrt{2\gamma})

(8.59)

where
P(x)=2\sqrt{2}n_{\bar{Q}}x^{1/8}
.

We will prove this result in several steps.
First, in section
8.3
we lower bound the dimension of the resource system.
In section
8.3
we translate this to a lower bound on the entropy of one side of any pure state resource.
Finally, we apply theorem
35
to translate this to a lower bound on the entanglement of formation.

Dimension lower bound

As our first step towards a proof of theorem
40
, we prove the following lemma which lower bounds the dimension of the resource system.

Lemma 41

Suppose
U_{AB}
has
\lambda
-controllable entanglement.
A non-local quantum computation which implements
U_{AB}^{\otimes m}
to within diamond norm
\epsilon
and uses a resource system
\Psi_{LR}
must have

\displaystyle n_{R}\geq m(\lambda_{1}-g_{1}(\epsilon))-g_{2}(\epsilon)

(8.60)

where
g_{1}(\epsilon),g_{2}(\epsilon)
are positive functions that go to
0
as
\epsilon\rightarrow 0
. More specifically, they are given by

\displaystyle g_{1}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=n_{\bar{Q}}(\eta_{\epsilon}+\eta_{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}),

\displaystyle g_{2}(\epsilon)

\displaystyle=H(\eta_{\epsilon})+H(\eta_{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}),

(8.61)

where
\eta_{x}=2\sqrt{x(1-x)}
, and
H(y)=(1+y)h\left(\frac{y}{1+y}\right)
.

Proof.
Suppose we have an NLQC protocol that implements
\tilde{U}
, with
\|\tilde{U}-U^{\otimes m}\|_{\diamond}\leq\epsilon
using a resource state
\Psi_{LR}
.
We take as input on the left one end of the maximally entangled state
{\left|{\Psi^{+}}\right\rangle}^{\otimes m}_{\bar{Q}A}
, and label the reference system by
\bar{Q}
.
The set up is shown in figure
8.1
; we will use the operation and system labels shown there.

We consider two scenarios.
First, consider inputting the
{\left|{\phi_{1}}\right\rangle}^{\otimes m}
state into the remaining input, labelled
B^{\prime}
.
We label the state resulting from a perfect implementation of
U^{\otimes m}
on this input as
\rho^{1}
, and from the imperfect implementation as
\sigma^{1}
.
In this case, a perfect implementation of
U^{\otimes m}
would lead to, by the definition of the controllable entanglement,

\displaystyle E_{f}(\bar{Q}:A)_{\rho^{1}}=\lambda_{1}m.

(8.62)

Since the protocol instead implements
\tilde{U}
which is close to
U^{\otimes m}
, we need to use the continuity bound of lemma
31
and we obtain

\displaystyle E_{f}(\bar{Q}:A)_{\rho^{1}}-E_{f}(\bar{Q}:A)_{\sigma^{1}}\leq m\,n_{\bar{Q}}\,\eta_{\epsilon}+H(\eta_{\epsilon}).

(8.63)

Using
E_{f}(\bar{Q}:A)_{\rho^{1}}=\lambda_{1}m
, we have then that the
A
output from
U_{AB}
is close to being
\lambda m
entangled with
R
,

\displaystyle E_{f}(\bar{Q}:A)_{\sigma^{1}}\geq m(\lambda-n_{\bar{Q}}\eta_{\epsilon})-H(\eta_{\epsilon}).

(8.64)

We can also observe that,

\displaystyle E_{f}(\bar{Q}:M_{1}M_{2})_{\sigma^{1}}\geq m(\lambda-n_{\bar{Q}}\eta_{\epsilon})-H(\eta_{\epsilon})

(8.65)

which follows from the previous line and the data processing inequality (lemma
30
) for the entanglement of formation.

Second, we input
{\left|{\phi^{2}}\right\rangle}^{\otimes m}
into
B
.
We label the state created in this case by
\rho^{2}
in the perfect case, and as
\sigma^{2}
in the imperfect case.
Recall that
\rho^{2}_{\bar{Q}A}
is separable.
Consider purifying the operation
\mathcal{W}^{L}_{M_{1}M_{2}\rightarrow A}
to a unitary
W^{L}_{M_{1}M_{2}\rightarrow AE}
.
This produces a state
\sigma_{QAE}
.
By lemma
75
, we can extend the
\sigma^{2},\rho^{2}
states to the
QABEX
Hilbert space (
X
is an additional purifying system) and obtain

\displaystyle\|\sigma^{2}_{\bar{Q}ABEX}-\rho^{2}_{\bar{Q}ABEX}\|_{1}\leq 2\sqrt{\epsilon}.

(8.66)

Since
\rho^{2}_{\bar{Q}AB}
is pure, the extension of
\rho^{2}
must be product across
\bar{Q}AB:EX
, so

\displaystyle\|\sigma^{2}_{\bar{Q}ABEX}-\rho^{2}_{\bar{Q}AB}\otimes\rho_{EX}\|_{1}\leq 2\sqrt{\epsilon}.

(8.67)

Next trace out
BX
,

\displaystyle\|\sigma_{\bar{Q}AE}^{2}-\rho_{\bar{Q}A}^{2}\otimes\rho_{E}\|_{1}\leq 2\sqrt{\epsilon}.

(8.68)

Now apply
(W^{L})^{\dagger}
to both states, which won’t change the trace distance, and choose an explicit decomposition of
\rho^{2}
into a convex sum over product states (recall that by assumption it is separable),

\displaystyle 2\sqrt{\epsilon}

\displaystyle\geq\left\|\sum_{i}p^{i}\rho^{i}_{\bar{Q}}\otimes(W^{L})^{\dagger}_{M_{1}M_{2}\rightarrow AE}(\rho^{i}_{A}\otimes\rho_{E})W^{L}_{M_{1}M_{2}\rightarrow AE}-(W^{L})^{\dagger}_{M_{1}M_{2}\rightarrow AE}(\sigma^{2}_{\bar{Q}A}\otimes\rho_{E})W^{L}_{M_{1}M_{2}\rightarrow AE}\right\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\left\|\sum_{i}p^{i}\rho^{i}_{\bar{Q}}\otimes\rho^{i}_{M_{1}M_{2}}-\sigma^{2}_{\bar{Q}M_{1}M_{2}}\right\|_{1}.

(8.69)

From this we also obtain that the state on
\sigma^{2}_{\bar{Q}M_{1}}
is close to separable, which using continuity of
E_{f}
gives,

\displaystyle E_{f}(\bar{Q}:M_{1})_{\sigma^{2}}\leq mn_{\bar{Q}}\eta_{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}+H(\eta_{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}).

(8.70)

Finally, notice that by causality the state on
\bar{Q}M_{1}
must be the same regardless of the input at
B
, so that

\displaystyle E_{f}(\bar{Q}:M_{1})_{\sigma^{1}}\leq m\,n_{\bar{Q}}\eta_{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}+H(\eta_{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}).

(8.71)

In words, we see that in the state
\sigma^{1}
systems
\bar{Q}:M_{1}
are close to separable, while
\bar{Q}:M_{1}M_{2}
is entangled.

Now we combine our statements so far to show that this can only occur when
R
is large enough,

\displaystyle m(\lambda-n_{\bar{Q}}\eta_{\epsilon})-H(\eta_{\epsilon})

\displaystyle\leq E_{f}(\bar{Q}:M_{1}M_{2})_{\sigma^{1}}\qquad\qquad\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{From eq. \eqref{eq:correct}}

\displaystyle\leq E_{f}(\bar{Q}:M_{1}RB^{\prime})_{\sigma^{1}}\qquad\qquad\,\,\,\text{From data processing}

\displaystyle=E_{f}(\bar{Q}:M_{1}R)_{\sigma^{1}}\qquad\qquad\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\,\text{Because $B^{\prime}$ is tensor product}

\displaystyle\leq E_{f}(\bar{Q}:M_{1})_{\sigma^{1}}+n_{R}\quad\,\,\,\,\,\,\qquad\text{From eq. \eqref{eq:SRcontinuity}}

\displaystyle=m\,n_{\bar{Q}}\eta_{2\sqrt{\epsilon}}+H(\eta_{2\sqrt{\epsilon}})+n_{R},\,\quad\,\,\,\,\text{From eq. \eqref{eq:smallonQM_1}.}

(8.72)

so we have that

\displaystyle n_{R}\geq m(\lambda-n_{\bar{Q}}\eta_{\epsilon}-n_{\bar{Q}}\eta_{2\sqrt{\epsilon}})-H(\eta_{\epsilon})-H(\eta_{2\sqrt{\epsilon}})

(8.73)

as claimed.

Entropy lower bound for any pure state resource

In section
8.3
we gave a lower bound on the number of qubits of resource system needed in an NLQC implementing a unitary
U_{AB}
with the controllable entanglement property.
In this section we translate this into a bound on the entanglement in the resource system, under the assumption that the resource system is pure, which we quantify using the entropy of one side of the resource state.
We treat this first in the case where the implementation of
U
is exact, then in the case where the implementation of
U
is approximate.
Our treatment of the approximate setting contains as a special case the exact one so in principle the exact case could be omitted, but the exact case is significantly simpler than the approximate one and conveys the key elements of the proof, so we retain it.

Exact case:
To upgrade our dimension lower bound to a lower bound on entropy, our approach is to make use of Schumacher compression
60
, stated in the next theorem.

Theorem 42

(Schumacher compression)

Suppose we have a quantum source which produces
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}_{LR}^{\otimes n}
.
Then, for all
\epsilon,\delta\in(0,1)
, there is a large enough
n
such that there exists a compression map
\mathcal{C}_{R^{n}\rightarrow M}
and decompression map
\mathcal{D}_{M\rightarrow R^{n}}
with

\displaystyle\||\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|^{\otimes n}-\mathcal{D}_{M\rightarrow R^{n}}\circ\mathcal{C}_{R^{n}\rightarrow M}(|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|^{\otimes n})\|_{1}\leq\epsilon

(8.74)

and where
\log d_{M}\leq(S(R)+\delta)n
.

We use this along with our lower bound on dimension, lemma
41
, to obtain a lower bound on entropy.
The basic idea is that the resource system
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}_{LR}
can be compressed to contain
S(R)m
qubits using Schumacher compression, but from our dimension bound we know the number of qubits must be
\lambda_{1}m
, so we must have
S(R)\geq\lambda_{1}
.
We give a more careful proof next.

Lemma 43

Suppose that an NLQC protocol implements
U_{AB}
exactly, using a pure resource state
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}_{LR}
, and where
U_{AB}
has
\lambda
-controllable entanglement. Then,
S(R)\geq\lambda
.

Proof.
Using Schumacher compression (theorem
42
), for any choice of
\epsilon,\delta>0
there is an
m
large enough, choice of compression channel
\mathcal{E}_{R^{m}\rightarrow M}
, and decompression channel
\mathcal{D}_{M\rightarrow R^{m}}
with
n_{M}\leq(S(R)+\delta)m
, such that if we define

\displaystyle\Psi_{L^{m}M}=\mathcal{E}_{R^{m}\rightarrow M}(|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|)

(8.75)

then

\displaystyle\left\||\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|^{\otimes m}_{LR}-\mathcal{D}_{M\rightarrow R^{m}}(\Psi_{L^{m}M})\right\|_{1}\leq\epsilon.

(8.76)

We define an NLQC protocol to implement
U^{\otimes m}
as follows.
The distributed resource state is taken to be
\Psi_{L^{m}M}
.
In the first set of operations, on the right, Bob applies
\mathcal{E}_{R^{m}\rightarrow M}
, leaving Alice and Bob sharing a state
\epsilon
-close to
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}^{\otimes m}
.
Next, they run
m
copies of the protocol, using the
m
copies of
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}
as resource states.
By the properties of the diamond norm distance, this will be
\epsilon
-close in diamond norm to an implementation of
U^{\otimes m}
.

Now we make use of lemma
41
, which tells us that

\displaystyle n_{M}\geq m(\lambda-g_{1}(\epsilon))-g_{2}(\epsilon)

(8.77)

But also, at large enough
m
,
(S(R)+\delta)m\geq n_{M}
, so that

\displaystyle S(R)\geq\lambda-\delta-g_{1}(\epsilon)-g_{2}(\epsilon)/m.

(8.78)

But we can choose
\epsilon
,
\delta
arbitrarily small while
m
becomes arbitrarily large, so this simplifies to
S(R)\geq\lambda
, as claimed.

Approximate case:
When the NLQC protocol implements
U
approximately, the asymptotic statement of Schumacher compression doesn’t suffice to obtain a lower bound.
The reason for this can be seen by considering equation (
8.78
).
There, the error
\epsilon
in the implementation of
U^{\otimes m}
comes from the approximation to the resource state appearing when decompressing from Schumacher’s scheme.
If each
U
implementation is approximate, there is a contribution to the error from each
U
, so we would replace
\epsilon\rightarrow\epsilon+\gamma\,m
where
\gamma
is the error in a single
U
implementation.
But the lower bound only applies when the (total) error has
\epsilon+\gamma m\in[0,1]
, so we don’t obtain a bound in the
m\rightarrow\infty
limit.
To remedy this, we will need to consider Schumacher compression for a finite number of copies of the input state.
This is addressed in
1
; we briefly recall one of their results here.

A compression protocol consists of a compression channel
\mathcal{C}_{A^{n}\rightarrow M}
and a decompression channel
\mathcal{D}_{M\rightarrow A^{n}}
.
We say the protocol is
\epsilon
-correct if the entanglement fidelity of the input
\rho^{\otimes n}
is
\epsilon
-close to the entanglement fidelity of the output,

\displaystyle F_{e}(\rho_{A}^{\otimes n},\mathcal{D}\circ\mathcal{C}(\rho_{A}^{\otimes n}))\geq 1-\epsilon.

(8.79)

We denote the minimal log-dimension of
M
needed to achieve
\epsilon
-correct compression on
n
copies of
\rho_{A}
by
M(n,\epsilon,\rho)
.

The value of
M(n,\epsilon,\rho)
is well understood.
To state the result, we define the function on density matrices

\displaystyle V(A)_{\rho}=\text{tr}(\rho_{A}\log^{2}\rho_{A})-\left(\text{tr}(\rho_{A}\log\rho_{A})\right)^{2}

(8.80)

and the function

\displaystyle\Phi^{-1}(x)=\sup\left\{z\in\mathbb{R}:\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\int_{-\infty}^{z}e^{-t^{2}/2}dt\leq x\right\}.

(8.81)

This is known as the
quantile
of the normal distribution; it expresses how far we need to integrate the normal distribution with variance 1 to reach a given value
x
.
The quantile of the normal distribution is defined on
(0,1)
and diverges as
x\rightarrow 0,1
.

Finally, we can state the following theorem, proven in
1
as theorem 3.

Theorem 44

(Schumacher compression at finite block length)

The minimal achievable value of
M(n,\epsilon,\rho)
in performing
\epsilon
-correct compression of the state
\rho_{A}
is

\displaystyle M(n,\epsilon,\rho)=nS(A)_{\rho}+\Phi^{-1}(\sqrt{1-\epsilon})\sqrt{nV(A)_{\rho}}+O(\log n)

(8.82)

We use this along with lemma
41
to obtain a lower bound on the entropy.

Theorem 45

Suppose that an NLQC protocol implements
U_{AB}
to within diamond norm distance
\gamma
, using a pure resource state
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}_{LR}
. Then, for small enough
\gamma
,

\displaystyle S(R)_{\psi}\geq\lambda-P(\gamma).

(8.83)

where
P(\gamma)=2\sqrt{2}n_{\bar{Q}}\gamma^{1/8}
.

Proof.
We consider an implementation of
U^{\otimes m}
, where we will choose
m
later.
Our implementation uses as a resource state a compressed version of
{\left|{\psi}\right\rangle}_{LR}^{\otimes m}
, that is we use

\displaystyle\Psi_{L^{m}M}=\mathcal{I}\otimes\mathcal{E}_{R_{n}\rightarrow M}(|\psi\rangle\!\langle\psi|^{\otimes m})

(8.84)

where
\mathcal{E}_{R^{n}\rightarrow M}
is an optimal compression channel.
The protocol proceeds by first having Bob decompress
M
into
R^{n}
, and then running
m
parallel implementations of
U
as before.
We use an
\epsilon
-correct compression protocol where we choose
\epsilon
later.
Since the compression protocol is
\epsilon
-correct, and each
U
implementation is
\gamma
-correct, by the properties of the diamond norm the implementation of
U^{\otimes m}
using the compressed resource state will be
\epsilon+\gamma\,m
correct.

Now we make use of lemma
41
, which tells us that

\displaystyle n_{M}\geq m(\lambda-g_{1}(\epsilon+\gamma m))-g_{2}(\epsilon+\gamma m).

(8.85)

Now use theorem
44
as a bound on
n_{M}
, and using that
V(R)_{\rho}\leq n_{R}^{2}
, we have

\displaystyle S(R)_{\psi}\geq\lambda_{1}-\Phi^{-1}(\sqrt{1-\epsilon})\frac{n_{R}}{\sqrt{m}}-g_{1}(\epsilon+\gamma m)-\frac{g_{2}(\epsilon+\gamma m)}{m}-O\left(\frac{\log m}{m}\right)

For intuition, notice that if we take
\gamma=0
we can maximize the lower bound by sending
\epsilon\rightarrow 0,m\rightarrow\infty
, in which case we recover
S(R)_{\psi}\geq\lambda
.
At non-zero
\gamma
however, sending
m\rightarrow\infty
would trivialize the lower bound.
To recover a good lower bound, we need to choose
\epsilon,m
in a way that depends on
\gamma
such that the lower bound approaches
\lambda
as
\gamma\rightarrow 0
.
We will achieve this with a simple choice by taking

\displaystyle\epsilon

\displaystyle=\gamma,\qquad m=\frac{1}{\sqrt{\gamma}}.

(8.86)

Inserting this above leads to the lower bound

\displaystyle S(R)_{\psi}\geq\lambda_{1}-\Phi^{-1}(\sqrt{1-\gamma})n_{R}\gamma^{1/4}-g_{1}(\gamma+\sqrt{\gamma})-\sqrt{\gamma}g_{2}(\gamma+\sqrt{\gamma})-O\left(\sqrt{\gamma}\log\gamma\right).

We can see that as
\gamma\rightarrow 0
this approaches the lower bound obtained in the exact case, so this bound is equal to that one plus terms that go to zero as
\gamma\rightarrow 0
.
To obtain the error terms, we expand in a series around
\gamma=0
, obtaining

\displaystyle S(R)_{\psi}\geq\lambda-2\sqrt{2}n_{\bar{Q}}\gamma^{1/8}-\tilde{O}(n_{R}\gamma^{1/4})-\tilde{O}(n_{\bar{Q}}\gamma^{1/4})\,\,\,\text{as}\,\,\,\gamma\rightarrow 0

(8.87)

where the
\tilde{O}
notation hides logarithmic factors.
For
\gamma
small enough, concretely
\gamma
such that
\gamma
much smaller than
\min\{1/n_{R}^{4},1/n_{\bar{Q}}^{4}\}
then, we obtain the lower bound

\displaystyle S(R)_{\psi}\geq\lambda_{1}-2\sqrt{2}n_{\bar{Q}}\gamma^{1/8}

(8.88)

as claimed.

Entanglement lower bound

We’ve given lower bounds on the entropy of one end of the resource system for any resource state that allows a unitary
U_{AB}
with controllable entanglement to be implemented as an NLQC.
Theorem
40
now follows as a corollary of the pure state entropy lower bound (theorem
45
) and our general purpose method of upgrading entropy to entanglement lower bounds, theorem
35
.

8.4
Parallel repetition

Suppose that we have a lower bound on the entanglement needed to implement a channel
\mathcal{N}_{AB}
as an NLQC, say of
E_{f}\geq\lambda
.
Does this mean that the channel
\mathcal{N}_{AB}^{\otimes n}
requires entanglement at least
n\lambda
?
This question is known as the
parallel repetition
question for an NLQC; similar questions appear in the context of non-local games and other settings.
We do not have a general understanding of when parallel repetition holds for an NLQC.
We can notice however that the lower bounds proven in this chapter, from controllable correlation and controllable entanglement, both have nice parallel repetition properties.

Let’s look first at the controllable correlation.

Corollary 46

Consider a unitary
G
with
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
-controllable correlation.
Then an (exact) implementation of
G^{\otimes n}
as an NLQC requires entanglement of formation in the resource state
\Psi
lower bounded by

\displaystyle E_{f}(L:R)\geq n\left(\frac{\lambda_{1}-\lambda_{2}}{2}\right)

(8.89)

Proof.
Suppose that
\phi_{1},\phi_{2},P_{\bar{Q}A}
can be used to show
G
has
(\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2})
-controllable correlation.
Then considering
G^{\otimes n}
, use the correlated state
P_{\bar{Q}A}^{\otimes n}
, and inputs
\phi_{1}^{\otimes n}
,
\phi_{2}^{\otimes n}
, we obtain
\lambda_{1}^{\prime}=n\lambda_{1}
,
\lambda_{2}^{\prime}=n\lambda_{2}
, which lead to the stated lower bound.

There is an open problem around parallel repetition for the controllable correlation lower bound in the noisy context.
In particular, we do not have a good lower bound on implementations of
(G^{\prime})^{\otimes n}
where
G^{\prime}
is a noisy version of
G
.

For the controllable entanglement, we also have a nice parallel repetition property.

Lemma 47

(Parallel repetition)

Suppose that a unitary
G
has
\lambda
-controllable entanglement.
Then
G^{\otimes n}
has
n\lambda
-controllable entanglement.

This follows because of additivity across tensor products of the entanglement of formation.
As with the controllable correlation case, it is an open problem to obtain parallel repetition for noisy implementations of
G
repeated
n
times.

8.5
History and further reading

The controllable correlation and controllable entanglement lower bounds were proven in
23
.
The lower bound on the simple routing task, which inspires these strategies, was first written down in an appendix of
11
, though the result was previously known as folklore.

Chapter 9
Lower bounds on
f
-routing

In the last chapter we made some progress on lower bounding the entanglement needed to implement unitaries as NLQCs.
We’ve seen that
f
-routing is a class of special importance to the theory of NLQC; it’s a good candidate QPV scheme, plays a special role in the
T
-depth based protocol, and is closely connected to the conditional disclosure of secrets (CDS) primitive studied in information-theoretic classical cryptography.
All of these applications motivate us to try and understand lower bounds on
f
-routing.

In this chapter we give two lower bound techniques for
f
-routing.
A key property we would like such lower bounds to have is that the cost of implementing
f
-routing should grow with the length of the classical strings given as input, call it
n
.
Our bounds have this property.
Unfortunately, our first bound achieves this at the expense of inducing a drawback — our entanglement lower bound only applies if the
f
-routing protocol is either perfectly correct when
f(x,y)=0
, or perfectly correct when
f(x,y)=1
.
Obtaining lower bounds on entanglement that grow (faster than logarithmically) with
n
even when allowing two-sided error is an important open problem.

We can get around this drawback of bounding only perfect protocols, but this comes at the expense of changing our focus from entanglement to quantum gates.
We prove polynomial lower bounds on the number of quantum gates needed to implement
f
-routing, even for some simple, explicit, functions.
These lower bounds hold even in the presence of noise.
While our primary focus is on entanglement cost in these lectures, from the standpoint of QPV at least it is interesting to also consider the gate cost.
Cryptographically the key idea is to have a resource that becomes large for the dishonest player but remains small for the honest player as a security parameter is increased.
In the context of QPV schemes based on
f
-routing, an honest player needs to compute a simple classical function then perform
O(1)
quantum gates, while a dishonest player who implements the NLQC we show needs
O(n)
gates.

9.1
Preliminaries

\mathcal{N}^{x}
\mathcal{M}^{y}
\mathcal{W}^{R}
\mathcal{W}^{L}
M_{0}
M_{1}^{\prime}
M_{0}^{\prime}
M_{1}
Q,x
y
A
B

Figure 9.1
:
General NLQC implementing an
f
-routing position-verification scheme. The first round operations are quantum channels that depend on the inputs
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
and
y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
. In the second round Alice and Bob apply channels mapping to qubit systems
A
,
B
. If
f(x,y)=0

A
should be maximally entangled with the reference
R
. If
f(x,y)=1
then
B
should be maximally entangled with
R
.

A general
f
-routing protocol is shown in figure
9.1
, where we also show the system labels we use in this section.
Note that we label
M_{0}M_{1}=M
,
M_{0}^{\prime}M_{1}^{\prime}=M^{\prime}
.
We will often consider taking the
Q
subsystem of a maximally entangled state
\Psi^{+}_{\bar{Q}Q}
as input to the protocol.
The density matrix
\rho_{\bar{Q}MM^{\prime}}
we refer to as the
mid-protocol density matrix
.

9.1.1
Communication complexity

In looking for lower bounds on
f
-routing, a likely source of inspiration is the field of communication complexity, which we first introduced in chapter
7
.
A useful object commonly studied in communication complexity is the
communication matrix
of a function
f:\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{n}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
.
This is defined to be a
2^{n}\times 2^{n}
matrix labelled
M_{f}
with rows labelled by
x
, columns labelled by
y
, and entries
f(x,y)
.
For instance, the equality function

\displaystyle EQ(x,y)=\begin{cases}0\,\,\,\text{if}\,\,\,x\neq y\\
1\,\,\,\text{if}\,\,\,x=y\end{cases}

(9.1)

has the identity matrix as its communication matrix.

The rank of the communication matrix
M_{f}
is related to the communication complexity of the function
f
.
For instance, let the minimal number of classical bits of communication needed to compute
f
(with probability 1) in the two-way communication model be denoted
\mathsf{R}
.
Then, we have that
47

\displaystyle\log(\text{rank}(M_{f}))\leq\mathsf{R}(f).

(9.2)

We will make use of a second notion of rank, which is called the non-deterministic rank.
This is defined by

\displaystyle\text{nrank}(f)=\min_{G\sim M_{f}}\text{rank}(G)

(9.3)

where the minimization is over all matrices
G
with the same zero entries as
M_{f}
, but arbitrary complex numbers in the non-zero entries.
The notion of
nrank
may seem a bit unwieldy, but we can notice that there are some simple cases where it can be lower bounded easily.
For instance the equality function has
\text{nrank}(M_{EQ})=2^{n}
, since
M_{EQ}
has non-zero entries on the diagonal and zero entries off the diagonal, so any
G
with the same zero’s must also be full rank.

Later in this chapter we will make use of the
simultaneous message passing
communication model, shown in figure
7.1(c)
.
We give a formal definition of this setting and the communication cost next.

Definition 48
(
\mathsf{R}\|
complexity)

Let
f:\{0,1\}^{n}\times\{0,1\}^{n}\rightarrow\{0,1\}
be a function, and let
\epsilon\in[0,1]
.
An
\mathsf{R}\|
protocol
P
for
f
consists of three algorithms Alice, Bob, and a referee.
Alice receives
x\in\{0,1\}^{n}
as input and outputs
m_{A}\in\{0,1\}^{*}
, Bob receives
y\in\{0,1\}^{n}
as input and outputs
m_{B}\in\{0,1\}^{*}
, and the referee receives
m_{A},m_{B}
and outputs a bit
c=P(x,y)
.
A protocol
P
is
\epsilon
-correct if

\displaystyle\forall(x,y):\Pr[P(x,y)=f(x,y)]\geq 1-\epsilon\kern 5.0pt.

The
\mathsf{R}\|_{\epsilon}
complexity of
f
is defined as follows

\mathsf{R}\|_{\epsilon}(f)=\min_{P:P\text{ is $\epsilon$-correct}}|{m_{A}}|+|{m_{B}}|\kern 5.0pt.

Similarly, we can define
\mathsf{R}\|^{*}_{\epsilon}(f)
for the case where Alice and Bob share entanglement.

We will relate gate cost in the
f
-routing scenario to the communication cost in the
\mathsf{R}\|^{*}_{\epsilon}(f)
model.
Because of that, it’s helpful to know that lower bounds on communication cost in this model are known for many functions, even some simple ones.
For instance, we have that the inner product function

\displaystyle IP_{n}(x,y)=\sum_{i=1}^{n}x_{i}y_{i}\,\,\text{mod}\,\,2

(9.4)

has
\mathsf{R}\|^{*}_{\epsilon}(IP_{n})=\Omega(n)
, and the disjointness function

\displaystyle DISJ_{n}(x,y)=\begin{cases}0\,\,\text{if}\,\,\exists\,\,i\,\,\,\text{s.t.}\,\,x_{i}=y_{i}=1\\
1\,\,\text{otherwise}\end{cases}

has
\mathsf{R}\|^{*}_{\epsilon}(DISJ_{n})=\Omega(\sqrt{n})
.

9.1.2
Structural features of
f
-routing protocols

To obtain lower bounds, we need to understand how the structure of the function
f
is reflected in the NLQC protocol, and specifically in the resource state
\Psi_{LR}
.
We are starting with conditions on the output systems, and we somehow want to wind these conditions backwards and see what they tell us about the resource state.
Towards this, we want to understand how the correctness requirements on the outputs translate to constraints on the protocol, and specifically on the mid-protocol density matrix.

Suppose we entangle the quantum input
Q
with a reference system
\bar{Q}
, then execute the first round operations.
This produces the mid-protocol density matrix
\rho_{\bar{Q}MM^{\prime}}(x,y)
.
Notice that if
f(x,y)=0
then the reference system
\bar{Q}
is maximally entangled with the output on the left, so must also be maximally entangled with the systems
M=M_{0}M_{1}
that are sent left.
Similarly, if
f(x,y)=1
then
\bar{Q}
must be maximally entangled with
M^{\prime}=M_{0}^{\prime}M_{1}^{\prime}
.
We state and prove this more carefully in the next lemma.

Lemma 49

Suppose an
f
-routing protocol is perfectly correct on zero instances, and
\epsilon_{1}<\epsilon_{1}^{*}
correct on
1
instances, where
\epsilon_{1}^{*}
depends on
d_{Q}
but is lower bounded by a constant. Then the mid-protocol state it produces,
\rho_{\bar{Q}M^{\prime}}
, satisfies
\rho_{\bar{Q}M^{\prime}}=\rho_{\bar{Q}}\otimes\rho_{M^{\prime}}
if and only if
f(x,y)=0
.

Proof.

By correctness on
0
instances, we have that

\displaystyle\text{when}\,\,\,f(x,y)=0,\,\,\,F(\mathcal{D}^{x,y}_{M^{\prime}\rightarrow Q}\circ\text{tr}_{M}\circ\mathcal{N}^{x,y}_{Q\rightarrow MM^{\prime}}(\Psi^{+}_{\bar{Q}Q}),\Psi^{+}_{\bar{Q}Q})=1

(9.5)

so that
f(x,y)=0
implies Alice can produce a Bell state
{\left|{\Psi^{+}}\right\rangle}_{\bar{Q}Q}
.
By data processing we have

\displaystyle 2n_{\bar{Q}}=I(\bar{Q}:Q)_{\Psi^{+}_{\bar{Q}Q}}\leq I(\bar{Q}:M)_{\rho_{\bar{Q}M}},

(9.6)

but we also have the inequality

\displaystyle I(\bar{Q}:M)_{\rho}+I(\bar{Q}:M^{\prime})_{\rho}\leq 2S(\bar{Q})

(9.7)

so that
I(\bar{Q}:M^{\prime})=0
, which implies

\displaystyle\rho_{\bar{Q}M^{\prime}}=\rho_{\bar{Q}}\otimes\rho_{M^{\prime}}

(9.8)

as needed.

Conversely, if
f(x,y)=1
we cannot have
\rho_{\bar{Q}M^{\prime}}=\rho_{\bar{Q}}\otimes\rho_{M^{\prime}}
.
To see why, first recall that
f
-routing which is
\epsilon_{1}
-correct on
1
instances has that there exists a family of channels
\{D^{x,y}_{M^{\prime}\rightarrow Q}\}_{x,y}
such that

\displaystyle\text{when}\,\,\,f(x,y)=1,\,\,\,F(\mathcal{D}^{x,y}_{M^{\prime}\rightarrow Q}\circ\text{tr}_{M}\circ\mathcal{N}^{x,y}_{Q\rightarrow MM^{\prime}}(\Psi^{+}_{\bar{Q}Q}),\Psi^{+}_{\bar{Q}Q})\geq 1-\epsilon_{1}.

(9.9)

Define

\displaystyle\sigma_{\bar{Q}Q}=\mathcal{D}^{x,y}_{M^{\prime}\rightarrow Q}(\rho_{\bar{Q}M^{\prime}})

(9.10)

and use that by data processing,

\displaystyle I(\bar{Q}:M^{\prime})_{\rho_{\bar{Q}M^{\prime}}}\geq I(\bar{Q}:Q)_{\sigma_{\bar{Q}Q}}.

(9.11)

But then by the Fuchs–van de Graaf inequalities
\sigma_{\bar{Q}Q}
is close in trace distance to
\Psi^{+}_{\bar{Q}Q}
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{2}||\Psi^{+}_{\bar{Q}Q}-\sigma_{\bar{Q}Q}||_{1}\leq\sqrt{\epsilon_{1}}

(9.12)

and by continuity of the mutual information (lemma
25
),

\displaystyle I(\bar{Q}:Q)_{\Psi^{+}}-I(\bar{Q}:Q)_{\sigma_{\bar{Q}Q}}\leq 2\sqrt{\epsilon_{1}}\log d_{\bar{Q}}+(1+\sqrt{\epsilon_{1}})h\left(\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon_{1}}}{1+\sqrt{\epsilon_{1}}}\right)

(9.13)

so that

\displaystyle 2\log d_{\bar{Q}}-2\sqrt{\epsilon_{1}}\log d_{\bar{Q}}-(1+\sqrt{\epsilon_{1}})h\left(\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon_{1}}}{1+\sqrt{\epsilon_{1}}}\right)\leq I(\bar{Q}:Q)_{\sigma}

(9.14)

We have that
\sigma
is not tensor product whenever the left hand side of the equation above is strictly positive.
For
d_{Q}=2
, this occurs for
\epsilon_{1}>\epsilon_{1}^{*}\approx 0.16
, and
\epsilon_{1}^{*}
approaches
1
as
d_{Q}\rightarrow\infty
.

This lemma is saying that
f(x,y)
is already fixed by the NLQC protocol after the
first
round operations, essentially because where the entanglement with
\bar{Q}
is going must already be decided before the communication or second round operations occur.

The above gives a property that the mid-protocol density matrix must have: it should be product across
\bar{Q}M^{\prime}
when
f(x,y)=0
.
We can also ask about how instantiations of
\rho_{MM^{\prime}}(x,y)
with differing values of
f(x,y)
are related to one another.
One intuition is that since
f(x,y)
is determined by the first round density matrix, we might expect
f(x,y)=0
instances of
\rho_{MM^{\prime}}(x,y)
to be distinguishable from
f(x,y)=1
instances.

To see this, we begin by defining sets of states for which the qubit can be produced on the left or right, respectively.

Definition 50

We define the 0-set
\tilde{S}_{0}^{\epsilon}
and 1-set
\tilde{S}_{1}^{\epsilon}
as

\displaystyle\tilde{S}_{0}^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\{\rho_{MM^{\prime}R}:\exists\,\mathcal{N}_{M\rightarrow Q}\,s.t.\,P(\mathcal{N}_{M\rightarrow Q}\circ\text{tr}_{M^{\prime}}(\rho_{\bar{Q}MM^{\prime}}),\Psi^{+}_{\bar{Q}Q})\leq\epsilon\}\kern 5.0pt,

\displaystyle\tilde{S}_{1}^{\epsilon}

\displaystyle=\{\rho_{MM^{\prime}R}:\exists\,\mathcal{N}_{M^{\prime}\rightarrow Q}\,s.t.\,P(\mathcal{N}_{M^{\prime}\rightarrow Q}\circ\text{tr}_{M}(\rho_{\bar{Q}MM^{\prime}}),\Psi^{+}_{\bar{Q}Q})\leq\epsilon\}\kern 5.0pt.

We would like to show that the sets
\tilde{S}_{0}^{\epsilon}
and
\tilde{S}_{1}^{\epsilon}
do not overlap when
\epsilon
is suitably small.
Intuitively, the non-overlap of these sets indicates that the entanglement with
R
has been brought to either Alice or Bob after the first round of operations – if there is a way to recover the entanglement on the left then there is not one on the right, and vice versa.
We first record the following lemma.

\phi^{0}
\phi^{1}
E
E
M
M^{\prime}
X
X
R
R
Q
Q
V_{M\rightarrow QE}
W^{\dagger}_{M\rightarrow QE}

(a)

E
E
M
M^{\prime}
X
X
\frac{1}{2}
\times
V_{M\rightarrow QE}
W^{\dagger}_{M\rightarrow QE}
Q
\phi^{0}
\phi^{1}

(b)

Figure 9.2
:
a) The inner product of
\psi^{0}
and
\psi^{1}
. The curved lines are maximally entangled qubit pairs. b) A rearrangement of the same inner product. The maximally entangled pairs have been straightened to a wire, and the normalization of
1/2
appears as an overall factor. The remaining object is again an inner product of two normalized states, now on a smaller Hilbert space, and is upper bounded by
1
.

Lemma 51

Let
\rho^{0}_{RMM^{\prime}}\in\tilde{S}_{0}^{\epsilon=0}
,
\rho^{1}_{RMM^{\prime}}\in\tilde{S}_{1}^{\epsilon=0}
.
Then

\displaystyle P(\rho^{0},\rho^{1})\geq\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\kern 5.0pt.

where we use the purified distance
P(\rho,\sigma)=\sqrt{1-(F(\rho,\sigma))^{2}}
.

Proof.

Consider purifications of
\tilde{\rho}^{0},\tilde{\rho}^{1}
.
Call these states
{\left|{\psi^{0}}\right\rangle}_{RMM^{\prime}X}
and
{\left|{\psi^{1}}\right\rangle}_{RMM^{\prime}X}
.
By purifying the channels appearing in the definitions of these sets, we have that there exist isometries
V_{M\rightarrow QE}
and
W_{M^{\prime}\rightarrow QE}
such that

\displaystyle\text{tr}_{M^{\prime}EX}\left(V_{M\rightarrow QE}|\psi^{0}\rangle\!\langle\psi^{0}|_{RMM^{\prime}X}V_{M\rightarrow QE}^{\dagger}\right)

\displaystyle=\Psi^{+}_{\bar{Q}Q}\kern 5.0pt,

\displaystyle\text{tr}_{M^{\prime}EX}\left(W_{M^{\prime}\rightarrow QE}|\psi^{1}\rangle\!\langle\psi^{1}|_{RMM^{\prime}X}W_{M^{\prime}\rightarrow QE}^{\dagger}\right)

\displaystyle=\Psi^{+}_{\bar{Q}Q}\kern 5.0pt.

This implies the existence of pure states
{\left|{\phi^{0}}\right\rangle}_{M^{\prime}EX}
,
{\left|{\phi^{1}}\right\rangle}_{MEX}
such that

\displaystyle{\left|{\psi^{0}}\right\rangle}_{RMM^{\prime}X}

\displaystyle=V_{M\rightarrow QE}^{\dagger}{\left|{\Psi^{+}}\right\rangle}_{RQ}\otimes{\left|{\phi^{0}}\right\rangle}_{M^{\prime}EX}\kern 5.0pt,

\displaystyle{\left|{\psi^{1}}\right\rangle}_{RMM^{\prime}X}

\displaystyle=W_{M^{\prime}\rightarrow QE}^{\dagger}{\left|{\Psi^{+}}\right\rangle}_{RQ}\otimes{\left|{\phi^{1}}\right\rangle}_{MEX}\kern 5.0pt.

All other purifications must be related by isometries
T^{0}_{X\rightarrow X^{\prime}}
,
T^{1}_{X\rightarrow X^{\prime}}
.
In
Fig.

9.2
, we give a simple tensor calculation that shows the inner product of all such purifications is always smaller than
1/2
, so that

\displaystyle F(\tilde{\rho}^{0},\tilde{\rho}^{1})=\max_{{\left|{\tilde{\psi}^{0}}\right\rangle},{\left|{\tilde{\psi}^{1}}\right\rangle}}\left|\langle\tilde{\psi}^{0}|\tilde{\psi}^{1}\rangle\right|\leq 1/2\kern 5.0pt.

This implies
P(\tilde{\rho}^{0},\tilde{\rho}^{1})\geq\sqrt{3}/2
as needed.

We can now prove the following.

Lemma 52

If
\epsilon<\frac{\sqrt{3}}{4}\approx 0.43
, then
\tilde{S}_{0}^{\epsilon}\cap\tilde{S}_{1}^{\epsilon}=\emptyset
.

Proof.

If
{\left|{\psi_{i}}\right\rangle}
is in
S_{i}^{\epsilon}
it must be
\epsilon
close in purified distance to a state in
S_{i}^{\epsilon=0}
.
Using this and lemma
51
we find that if
{\left|{\psi_{0}}\right\rangle}\in\tilde{S}_{0}^{\epsilon}
and
{\left|{\psi_{1}}\right\rangle}\in\tilde{S}_{1}^{\epsilon}
, then

\displaystyle P({\left|{\psi_{0}}\right\rangle},{\left|{\psi_{1}}\right\rangle})\geq\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}-2\epsilon\kern 5.0pt.

Assuming
\epsilon<\frac{\sqrt{3}}{4}
we find that the purified distance is strictly positive, and hence the sets do not overlap.

This lemma is giving some constraints on the geometry of the set of states
\rho_{MM^{\prime}}(x,y)
: 0 instances are far from 1 instances.

9.2
Rank lower bound

We’re now ready to develop our entanglement lower bound for
f
-routing.
In this section we will focus on
f
-routing with
\epsilon_{0}=0
,
\epsilon_{1}\geq 0
, so that the protocol is perfectly correct on zero instances of
f(x,y)
, or with
\epsilon_{0}>0
and
\epsilon_{1}=0
, so that the protocol is perfectly correct on
1
instances.
We define the entanglement cost of an
f
-routing protocol to be the logarithm of the minimal Schmidt rank of any resource system which can be used to perform the
f
-routing task.
In notation, we define
\mathsf{FR}_{0}(f)
to be entanglement cost for
f
-routing with
\epsilon_{0}=0
,
\epsilon_{1}=0.05
, and
\mathsf{FR}_{1}(f)
to be the entanglement cost when
\epsilon_{0}=0.05
,
\epsilon_{1}=0
.

Note that the Schmidt rank is a somewhat bad measure of the entanglement cost: we can deform a state (in trace distance) slightly yet make a large change to the Schmidt rank.
This is unfortunately a limitation of our methods so far.
If we assume the resource system consists of
n
EPR pairs, then the log Schmidt rank is equal to
n
.

In lemma
49
we saw that the mid-protocol density matrix in an
f
-routing protocol,
\rho_{\bar{Q}MM^{\prime}}(x,y)
has
\rho_{\bar{Q}M^{\prime}}(x,y)=\rho_{\bar{Q}}\otimes\rho_{M^{\prime}}(x,y)
if and only if
f(x,y)=0
.
To prove our rank lower bound, we will show that
\rho_{\bar{Q}M^{\prime}}(x,y)
is constrained to be “simple” in a way that is constrained by the amount of entanglement in the resource state, and that consequently to have the density matrix be product for only the right values of
(x,y)
, it needs to have some lower bounded amount of entanglement.

To understand how this product structure in the mid-protocol density matrix relates to the entanglement in resource state, we define what we call a
structure function
for a protocol.

Definition 53

Given an
f
-routing protocol with mid-protocol density matrix
\rho_{\bar{Q}MM^{\prime}}
, define the
structure function

g(x,y)
according to

\displaystyle g(x,y)=\text{tr}\left(\rho_{\bar{Q}M^{\prime}}-\frac{\mathcal{I}}{d_{\bar{Q}}}\otimes\rho_{M^{\prime}}\right)^{2}.

(9.15)

Note that we can also phrase this definition in terms of the Frobenius norm,
\|A\|_{F}=\sqrt{\text{tr}(A^{\dagger}A)}
.

We claim that
g(x,y)
captures some aspect of the structure in the function
f
which must be present in a correct
f
-routing protocol.
More concretely we have the following.

Lemma 54

In a perfectly correct
f
-routing protocol, the structure function
g(x,y)
is zero if and only if
f(x,y)=0
.

Proof.

This follows because
g(x,y)=0
is zero if and only if
\rho_{\bar{Q}M^{\prime}}=\frac{\mathcal{I}}{d_{\bar{Q}}}\otimes\rho_{M^{\prime}}
, and lemma
49
shows we have this tensor product form if and only if
f(x,y)=0
.

Our next job is to relate the function
g(x,y)
to the entanglement available to Alice and Bob.
We prove the following lemma.

Lemma 55

An
f
-routing protocol that uses a resource system with Schmidt rank
d_{E}
has a structure function of the form

\displaystyle g(x,y)=\sum_{I=1}^{d_{E}^{4}}f_{I}(x)f_{I}^{\prime}(y)\kern 5.0pt.

Proof.

From the general form of a non-local quantum computation protocol, the density matrix
\rho_{\bar{Q}M_{0}^{\prime}M_{1}^{\prime}}(x,y)
can be expressed as

\displaystyle\rho_{\bar{Q}M_{0}^{\prime}M_{1}^{\prime}}=\mathcal{N}^{x}_{QL\rightarrow M_{0}^{\prime}}\otimes\mathcal{M}^{y}_{\bar{Q}\rightarrow M_{1}^{\prime}}(\Psi^{+}_{\bar{Q}Q}\otimes|\Psi\rangle\!\langle\Psi|_{LR})\kern 5.0pt.

We will write
{\left|{\Psi}\right\rangle}_{LR}
in the Schmidt basis,

\displaystyle{\left|{\Psi}\right\rangle}_{LR}=\sum_{i=1}^{d_{E}}{\left|{i}\right\rangle}_{L}{\left|{i}\right\rangle}_{R}\kern 5.0pt,

with un-normalized vectors
{\left|{i}\right\rangle}_{L},{\left|{i}\right\rangle}_{R}
.
Then we get
